========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess the main thing is I’ve been really stuck in my own head lately. I work as a computer systems analyst, and I’ve been in a more senior-type role, or at least people seem to think I can handle that level of responsibility, and I just keep feeling like… like I tricked everyone somehow.

I keep beating myself up, thinking I’m not actually “senior material,” that I’m not as capable as they think I am, and that eventually they’re going to realize I can’t handle as much as they believed. It’s not just a passing thought either—I keep turning it over and over, replaying things I said or decisions I made, and I end up feeling guilty, like I’ve misrepresented myself even though I didn’t mean to. That’s really why I came in.

Yeah, somewhat. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped doing the job or anything—I’m still showing up, still getting my work done, still answering messages and going to meetings. But I’ve noticed I’m a lot less… confident, I guess, in how I move through things.

Like, I’ll finish something and then go back over it more than I normally would, sometimes several times, because I don’t trust that I caught everything or that my judgment was right the first time. Even with decisions I’ve made plenty of times before, I’ll hesitate and sort of second-guess the reasoning, like, “Was that actually the right call, or am I just pretending I know what I’m doing?” So it slows me down a bit, and it makes everything feel heavier.

And outside of work, I’ve kind of pulled back from people—friends, family—not because they did anything, but because I don’t really trust my mood or my judgment lately. I worry I’ll say something off, or I won’t be present, or I’ll make things awkward. So I’ve been keeping more to myself. It’s not like my whole life has fallen apart, but I can feel myself withdrawing and overchecking things more than I used to.

reports and started messaging clients and coworkers with ideas before anyone asked, like I was trying to prove I could run the whole thing myself.\n- key: S041\n  name: Painful_Consequence_Behavior\n gear all in a short run, and later I looked at the card bill wondering what I was thinking.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m still making it to work, / 43 / computer_systems_analyst / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess the main thing is I’ve been really stuck in my own head lately. I work as a computer systems analyst, and I’ve been in a more senior-type role, or at least people seem to think I can handle that level of responsibility, and I just keep feeling like… like I tricked everyone somehow.\n\nI keep beating myself up, thinking I’m not actually “senior material,” that I’m not as capable as they think I am, and that eventually they’re going to realize I can’t handle as much as they believed. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped doing the job or anything—I’m still showing up, still getting my work done, still answering messages and going to meetings. Even with decisions I’ve made plenty of times before, I’ll hesitate and sort of second-guess the reasoning, like, “Was that actually the right call, or am I just pretending I know what I’m doing?” So it slows me down a bit, and it makes everything feel heavier.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve kind of pulled back from people—friends, family—not because they did anything, but because I don’t really trust my mood or my judgment lately. I worry I’ll say something off, or I won’t be present, or I’ll make things awkward. So I’ve been keeping more to myself. It’s not like my whole life has fallen apart, but I can feel myself withdrawing and overchecking things more than I used to.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that your anxiety or worry tends to come and go depending on specific stressors, or does it feel more constant, like it’s always lingering in the background even when things are going okay?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not totally sure how to separate that, honestly. It feels like it’s tied to work a lot of the time, or at least that’s what I notice most clearly, because that’s where the “I’m going to be found out” kind of feeling shows up.

Some days it’s more like a low hum, and I can go through the day and function, and other days something small will kick it up, like an email from someone higher up or having to give an opinion, and then I start turning it over again.

So… I don’t know if I’d call it constant exactly, because there are moments when I’m distracted or things feel okay. But it’s also not only when there’s a clear crisis. It sort of lingers, especially around the job stuff.

/ 43 / computer_systems_analyst / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find that these kinds of worries extend beyond work? Episode Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: Looking back, the low, guilty periods have been hanging around off and on for about eleven months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / computer_systems_analyst / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain tasks, responsibilities, or even interactions at work because of these worries about being seen as inadequate or a fraud?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped doing the job or anything—I’m still showing up, still getting my work done, still answering messages and going to meetings. But I’ve noticed I’m a lot less… confident, I guess, in how I move through things.\n\nLike, I’ll finish something and then go back over it more than I normally would, sometimes several times, because I don’t trust that I caught everything or that my judgment was right the first time. Not always at the same volume, I guess. Some days it’s more like a low hum, and I can go through the day and function, and other days something small will kick it up, like an email from someone higher up or having to give an opinion, and then I start turning it over again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it constant exactly, because there are moments when I’m distracted or things feel okay. But it’s also not only when there’s a clear crisis. It sort of lingers, especially around the job stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed changes in your mood, like feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, especially alongside these worries about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. Like I’m going through the motions at work and then coming home and doing the basic things I’m supposed to do, but everything feels kind of dulled out.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is like the color’s been turned down on things. I’m still functioning, I’m still doing what I need to do, but there’s not much brightness to it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I keep beating myself up over this idea that I’m not really senior material at work, like I somehow got put in a position people think I can handle, but I’ve fooled them into believing I’m more capable than I actually am.

And the thing is, if someone else described the same situation to me, I’d probably tell them they’re being way too hard on themselves. I can recognize that, logically. But when it’s me, it feels different. I keep thinking, “You should know more by now,” or “You shouldn’t need to double-check this,” or “At your age and at this level, you shouldn’t feel this unsure.” It turns into this running commentary in my head where I’m not just worried about making a mistake—I’m judging myself for even having the worry.

So yeah, guilty or worthless… maybe “worthless” sounds strong, but there are definitely moments where I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people have put in me, and like I’m taking up a role that should belong to someone more solid or more naturally confident. It’s embarrassing to say out loud, because nobody has really said that to me directly. It’s more like I’ve decided it about myself and then I keep looking for proof.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can usually attach it to work if I think long enough, but it’s not always like I did one specific thing wrong and then I feel guilty. A lot of the time it’s more like this general feeling that I’m not what people think I am.

That’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep beating myself up over this idea that I’m not really senior material, like I somehow fooled everyone into thinking I can handle more responsibility than I actually can. And nobody is necessarily saying that to me. It’s more internal. I’ll just be sitting there, even if the day is going basically fine, and I’ll start thinking, “You shouldn’t be in this role,” or “They’re going to realize you’re not as capable as they thought.”

So yes, it can feel like it’s just there regardless of what’s happening around me. Not every second, but enough that it feels like I’m carrying it around.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. It feels like I can usually find a reason if I look for one long enough, especially something work-related or some interaction I’m replaying in my head. But I don’t know if that means it was actually triggered by that, or if my brain just sort of grabs onto whatever’s nearby and uses it as the explanation.

There are times where I’ll just feel keyed up or uneasy and then I start scanning for why. Like, “Did I forget something? Is there an email I missed? Did I sound stupid in that meeting?” And sometimes there is a specific thing, but other times it’s more vague than that. It’s not always like, “This happened, so now I’m anxious.” Sometimes the worry seems to show up first, and then I attach it to something after the fact.

So… I guess I’d say it’s not always tied to one clear event, but I also have a hard time saying it comes out of nowhere, because I’m pretty good at making it about something once it’s there.

It feels like it’s tied to work a lot of the time, or at least that’s what I notice most clearly, because that’s where the “I’m going to be found out” kind of feeling shows up.\n\nBut then even when nothing specific is happening—like no big meeting, no deadline, no one criticizing me—it can still kind of sit there in the background. Not always at the same volume, I guess. Some days it’s more like a low hum, and I can go through the day and function, and other days something small will kick it up, like an email from someone higher up or having to give an opinion, and then I start turning it over again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it constant exactly, because there are moments when I’m distracted or things feel okay. But it’s also not only when there’s a clear crisis. It sort of lingers, especially around the job stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed changes in your mood, like feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, especially alongside these worries about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. It’s not like I wake up every single morning thinking, “I’m hopeless,” exactly, but most days lately there’s been this heavy, low feeling sitting on me. Like I’m going through the motions at work and then coming home and doing the basic things I’m supposed to do, but everything feels kind of dulled out.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is like the color’s been turned down on things. I’m still functioning, I’m still doing what I need to do, but there’s not much brightness to it. And with the work worries layered on top, it’s like even normal parts of the day feel heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or worthless, even when there’s no clear reason or when others wouldn’t see the situation the same way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I keep beating myself up over this idea that I’m not really senior material at work, like I somehow got put in a position people think I can handle, but I’ve fooled them into believing I’m more capable than I actually am.\n\nAnd the thing is, if someone else described the same situation to me, I’d probably tell them they’re being way too hard on themselves. I can recognize that, logically. But when it’s me, it feels different. I keep thinking, “You should know more by now,” or “You shouldn’t need to double-check this,” or “At your age and at this level, you shouldn’t feel this unsure.” It turns into this running commentary in my head where I’m not just worried about making a mistake—I’m judging myself for even having the worry.\n\nSo yeah, guilty or worthless… maybe “worthless” sounds strong, but there are definitely moments where I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people have put in me, and like I’m taking up a role that should belong to someone more solid or more naturally confident. It’s embarrassing to say out loud, because nobody has really said that to me directly. It’s more like I’ve decided it about myself and then I keep looking for proof.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling guilty or worthless even when you haven’t done anything specific to cause those feelings, like they’re just there regardless of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can usually attach it to work if I think long enough, but it’s not always like I did one specific thing wrong and then I feel guilty. A lot of the time it’s more like this general feeling that I’m not what people think I am.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep beating myself up over this idea that I’m not really senior material, like I somehow fooled everyone into thinking I can handle more responsibility than I actually can. And nobody is necessarily saying that to me. It’s more internal. I’ll just be sitting there, even if the day is going basically fine, and I’ll start thinking, “You shouldn’t be in this role,” or “They’re going to realize you’re not as capable as they thought.”\n\nSo yes, it can feel like it’s just there regardless of what’s happening around me. Not every second, but enough that it feels like I’m carrying it around.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, dulled-out mood, and self-criticism — and has it been present most days for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… so, looking back, I’d say this has been hanging around off and on for about eleven months now. Not always at the exact same intensity, and there have been stretches where I’ve felt more like myself or at least more functional, but the low feeling and the guilt-y, self-critical stuff keeps coming back.\n\nAs far as whether it’s been most days for two weeks at a time… yeah, I think so. I’m not great at marking it on a calendar, but there have definitely been periods where for a couple weeks or more, most days had that same heaviness to them. It’s like I could still get through work and do what I needed to do, so part of me kept dismissing it, but emotionally it’s been there in the background a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry often comes up even when there’s no specific stressor or trigger, or does it always seem to be tied to a particular situation or event?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. It feels like I can usually find a reason if I look for one long enough, especially something work-related or some interaction I’m replaying in my head. But I don’t know if that means it was actually triggered by that, or if my brain just sort of grabs onto whatever’s nearby and uses it as the explanation.\n\nThere are times where I’ll just feel keyed up or uneasy and then I start scanning for why. Like, “Did I forget something? Is there an email I missed? Did I sound stupid in that meeting?” And sometimes there is a specific thing, but other times it’s more vague than that. It’s not always like, “This happened, so now I’m anxious.” Sometimes the worry seems to show up first, and then I attach it to something after the fact.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say it’s not always tied to one clear event, but I also have a hard time saying it comes out of nowhere, because I’m pretty good at making it about something once it’s there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It’s not always like, “This happened, so now I’m anxious.” Sometimes the worry seems to show up first, and then I attach it to something after the fact.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say it’s not always tied to one clear event, but I also have a hard time saying it comes out of nowhere, because I’m pretty good at making it about something once it’s there.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that your anxiety or worry often comes up even when there's no specific stressor or trigger related to work or other situations, or does it always seem to be tied to a particular situation or event?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / computer_systems_analyst / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… I may have already sort of answered this, but I’m honestly still not completely sure how to tell the difference.

It doesn’t feel neatly tied to one thing every time. Like with work, that’s the easiest place my mind goes, because there’s always something I can point to if I try hard enough — an email, a meeting, whether I sounded competent, whether I missed some detail. But sometimes the uneasy feeling is there first, and then I start looking around for the reason. So I’ll kind of assign it to something afterward.

I wouldn’t say it always comes out of nowhere, though. That feels too strong, because once I’m worried, I can usually find some situation to connect it to. But I also can’t say it’s always because of one clear event. It’s more like this background hum sometimes, and then my brain picks a target.

Even with decisions I’ve made plenty of times before, I’ll hesitate and sort of second-guess the reasoning, like, “Was that actually the right call, or am I just pretending I know what I’m doing?” So it slows me down a bit, and it makes everything feel heavier.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve kind of pulled back from people—friends, family—not because they did anything, but because I don’t really trust my mood or my judgment lately. I worry I’ll say something off, or I won’t be present, or I’ll make things awkward. So I’ve been keeping more to myself. It’s not like my whole life has fallen apart, but I can feel myself withdrawing and overchecking things more than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry tends to come and go depending on specific stressors, or does it feel more constant, like it’s always lingering in the background even when things are going okay?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that, honestly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, the sleep part is definitely there. I don’t know if I’d say it happens every single night, but when I’m more keyed up or beating myself up about things, sleep gets kind of messed up.

Lately it’s been one of two versions. Either I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be awake — and my brain immediately starts running through client tickets or things I might’ve missed, and then I can’t really get back to sleep. It’s not even productive thinking, it’s just the same stuff looping.

Or I’ll technically sleep enough hours, like on paper it should’ve been fine, but I wake up still feeling worn out, like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. So yeah, sleep has been tied into it, especially when I’m anxious or being hard on myself.

Some days it’s more like a low hum, and I can go through the day and function, and other days something small will kick it up, like an email from someone higher up or having to give an opinion, and then I start turning it over again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it constant exactly, because there are moments when I’m distracted or things feel okay. But it’s also not only when there’s a clear crisis. It sort of lingers, especially around the job stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed changes in your mood, like feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, especially alongside these worries about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. It’s not like I wake up every single morning thinking, “I’m hopeless,” exactly, but most days lately there’s been this heavy, low feeling sitting on me. Like I’m going through the motions at work and then coming home and doing the basic things I’m supposed to do, but everything feels kind of dulled out.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is like the color’s been turned down on things. I’m still functioning, I’m still doing what I need to do, but there’s not much brightness to it. And with the work worries layered on top, it’s like even normal parts of the day feel heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or worthless, even when there’s no clear reason or when others wouldn’t see the situation the same way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I keep beating myself up over this idea that I’m not really senior material at work, like I somehow got put in a position people think I can handle, but I’ve fooled them into believing I’m more capable than I actually am.\n\nAnd the thing is, if someone else described the same situation to me, I’d probably tell them they’re being way too hard on themselves. I can recognize that, logically. But when it’s me, it feels different. I keep thinking, “You should know more by now,” or “You shouldn’t need to double-check this,” or “At your age and at this level, you shouldn’t feel this unsure.” It turns into this running commentary in my head where I’m not just worried about making a mistake—I’m judging myself for even having the worry.\n\nSo yeah, guilty or worthless… maybe “worthless” sounds strong, but there are definitely moments where I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people have put in me, and like I’m taking up a role that should belong to someone more solid or more naturally confident. It’s embarrassing to say out loud, because nobody has really said that to me directly. It’s more like I’ve decided it about myself and then I keep looking for proof.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling guilty or worthless even when you haven’t done anything specific to cause those feelings, like they’re just there regardless of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can usually attach it to work if I think long enough, but it’s not always like I did one specific thing wrong and then I feel guilty. A lot of the time it’s more like this general feeling that I’m not what people think I am.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep beating myself up over this idea that I’m not really senior material, like I somehow fooled everyone into thinking I can handle more responsibility than I actually can. And nobody is necessarily saying that to me. It’s more internal. I’ll just be sitting there, even if the day is going basically fine, and I’ll start thinking, “You shouldn’t be in this role,” or “They’re going to realize you’re not as capable as they thought.”\n\nSo yes, it can feel like it’s just there regardless of what’s happening around me. Not every second, but enough that it feels like I’m carrying it around.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, dulled-out mood, and self-criticism — and has it been present most days for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… so, looking back, I’d say this has been hanging around off and on for about eleven months now. Not always at the exact same intensity, and there have been stretches where I’ve felt more like myself or at least more functional, but the low feeling and the guilt-y, self-critical stuff keeps coming back.\n\nAs far as whether it’s been most days for two weeks at a time… yeah, I think so. I’m not great at marking it on a calendar, but there have definitely been periods where for a couple weeks or more, most days had that same heaviness to them. It’s like I could still get through work and do what I needed to do, so part of me kept dismissing it, but emotionally it’s been there in the background a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry often comes up even when there’s no specific stressor or trigger, or does it always seem to be tied to a particular situation or event?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. It feels like I can usually find a reason if I look for one long enough, especially something work-related or some interaction I’m replaying in my head. But I don’t know if that means it was actually triggered by that, or if my brain just sort of grabs onto whatever’s nearby and uses it as the explanation.\n\nThere are times where I’ll just feel keyed up or uneasy and then I start scanning for why. Like, “Did I forget something? Is there an email I missed? Did I sound stupid in that meeting?” And sometimes there is a specific thing, but other times it’s more vague than that. It’s not always like, “This happened, so now I’m anxious.” Sometimes the worry seems to show up first, and then I attach it to something after the fact.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say it’s not always tied to one clear event, but I also have a hard time saying it comes out of nowhere, because I’m pretty good at making it about something once it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry often comes up even when there's no specific stressor or trigger related to work or other situations, or does it always seem to be tied to a particular situation or event?\n\nPatient: I think… I may have already sort of answered this, but I’m honestly still not completely sure how to tell the difference.\n\nIt doesn’t feel neatly tied to one thing every time. Like with work, that’s the easiest place my mind goes, because there’s always something I can point to if I try hard enough — an email, a meeting, whether I sounded competent, whether I missed some detail. But sometimes the uneasy feeling is there first, and then I start looking around for the reason. So I’ll kind of assign it to something afterward.\n\nI wouldn’t say it always comes out of nowhere, though. That feels too strong, because once I’m worried, I can usually find some situation to connect it to. But I also can’t say it’s always because of one clear event. It’s more like this background hum sometimes, and then my brain picks a target.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice physical symptoms, like restlessness, muscle tension, or trouble sleeping, especially when you're feeling more anxious or self-critical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the sleep part is definitely there. I don’t know if I’d say it happens every single night, but when I’m more keyed up or beating myself up about things, sleep gets kind of messed up.\n\nLately it’s been one of two versions. Either I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be awake — and my brain immediately starts running through client tickets or things I might’ve missed, and then I can’t really get back to sleep. It’s not even productive thinking, it’s just the same stuff looping.\n\nOr I’ll technically sleep enough hours, like on paper it should’ve been fine, but I wake up still feeling worn out, like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. So yeah, sleep has been tied into it, especially when I’m anxious or being hard on myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. Work is the thing I notice most because it’s concrete and easy to point to. Like I’m going through the motions at work and then coming home and doing the basic things I’m supposed to do, but everything feels kind of dulled out.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is like the color’s been turned down on things. I’m still functioning, I’m still doing what I need to do, but there’s not much brightness to it. And with the work worries layered on top, it’s like even normal parts of the day feel heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or worthless, even when there’s no clear reason or when others wouldn’t see the situation the same way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I keep beating myself up over this idea that I’m not really senior material at work, like I somehow got put in a position people think I can handle, but I’ve fooled them into believing I’m more capable than I actually am.\n\nAnd the thing is, if someone else described the same situation to me, I’d probably tell them they’re being way too hard on themselves. I can recognize that, logically. But when it’s me, it feels different. I keep thinking, “You should know more by now,” or “You shouldn’t need to double-check this,” or “At your age and at this level, you shouldn’t feel this unsure.” It turns into this running commentary in my head where I’m not just worried about making a mistake—I’m judging myself for even having the worry.\n\nSo yeah, guilty or worthless… maybe “worthless” sounds strong, but there are definitely moments where I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people have put in me, and like I’m taking up a role that should belong to someone more solid or more naturally confident. It’s embarrassing to say out loud, because nobody has really said that to me directly. It’s more like I’ve decided it about myself and then I keep looking for proof.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling guilty or worthless even when you haven’t done anything specific to cause those feelings, like they’re just there regardless of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can usually attach it to work if I think long enough, but it’s not always like I did one specific thing wrong and then I feel guilty. A lot of the time it’s more like this general feeling that I’m not what people think I am.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep beating myself up over this idea that I’m not really senior material, like I somehow fooled everyone into thinking I can handle more responsibility than I actually can. And nobody is necessarily saying that to me. It’s more internal. I’ll just be sitting there, even if the day is going basically fine, and I’ll start thinking, “You shouldn’t be in this role,” or “They’re going to realize you’re not as capable as they thought.”\n\nSo yes, it can feel like it’s just there regardless of what’s happening around me. Not every second, but enough that it feels like I’m carrying it around.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, dulled-out mood, and self-criticism — and has it been present most days for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… so, looking back, I’d say this has been hanging around off and on for about eleven months now. Not always at the exact same intensity, and there have been stretches where I’ve felt more like myself or at least more functional, but the low feeling and the guilt-y, self-critical stuff keeps coming back.\n\nAs far as whether it’s been most days for two weeks at a time… yeah, I think so. I’m not great at marking it on a calendar, but there have definitely been periods where for a couple weeks or more, most days had that same heaviness to them. It’s like I could still get through work and do what I needed to do, so part of me kept dismissing it, but emotionally it’s been there in the background a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry often comes up even when there’s no specific stressor or trigger, or does it always seem to be tied to a particular situation or event?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. It feels like I can usually find a reason if I look for one long enough, especially something work-related or some interaction I’m replaying in my head. But I don’t know if that means it was actually triggered by that, or if my brain just sort of grabs onto whatever’s nearby and uses it as the explanation.\n\nThere are times where I’ll just feel keyed up or uneasy and then I start scanning for why. Like, “Did I forget something? Is there an email I missed? Did I sound stupid in that meeting?” And sometimes there is a specific thing, but other times it’s more vague than that. It’s not always like, “This happened, so now I’m anxious.” Sometimes the worry seems to show up first, and then I attach it to something after the fact.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say it’s not always tied to one clear event, but I also have a hard time saying it comes out of nowhere, because I’m pretty good at making it about something once it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry often comes up even when there's no specific stressor or trigger related to work or other situations, or does it always seem to be tied to a particular situation or event?\n\nPatient: I think… I may have already sort of answered this, but I’m honestly still not completely sure how to tell the difference.\n\nIt doesn’t feel neatly tied to one thing every time. Like with work, that’s the easiest place my mind goes, because there’s always something I can point to if I try hard enough — an email, a meeting, whether I sounded competent, whether I missed some detail. But sometimes the uneasy feeling is there first, and then I start looking around for the reason. So I’ll kind of assign it to something afterward.\n\nI wouldn’t say it always comes out of nowhere, though. That feels too strong, because once I’m worried, I can usually find some situation to connect it to. But I also can’t say it’s always because of one clear event. It’s more like this background hum sometimes, and then my brain picks a target.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice physical symptoms, like restlessness, muscle tension, or trouble sleeping, especially when you're feeling more anxious or self-critical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the sleep part is definitely there. I don’t know if I’d say it happens every single night, but when I’m more keyed up or beating myself up about things, sleep gets kind of messed up.\n\nLately it’s been one of two versions. Either I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be awake — and my brain immediately starts running through client tickets or things I might’ve missed, and then I can’t really get back to sleep. It’s not even productive thinking, it’s just the same stuff looping.\n\nOr I’ll technically sleep enough hours, like on paper it should’ve been fine, but I wake up still feeling worn out, like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. So yeah, sleep has been tied into it, especially when I’m anxious or being hard on myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about other areas of your life outside of work, like your health, relationships, or things that might happen in the future, even when there’s no immediate reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. Work is the thing I notice most because it’s concrete and easy to point to. With stuff outside of work — like health or relationships or the future — I don’t immediately think, “Yes, that’s a big pattern,” but I also don’t know if I’ve been paying close enough attention to separate it out.\n\nI mean, I can worry about those things like anyone does, but I’m not sure I’d call it excessive, or at least I don’t have a specific example jumping out at me right now. It’s more that when I’m already uneasy, my mind can kind of look for something to attach to, and work is usually the easiest target. Outside of work, it’s less obvious to me. So… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed a strong pattern there, but I’m not totally certain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I think my answer is still kind of the same, though I’m sorry if I’m circling around it.

I don’t really notice a clear pattern of worrying a lot about health or relationships or future stuff when there’s no reason. Not in the way I notice it with work. Even with decisions I’ve made plenty of times before, I’ll hesitate and sort of second-guess the reasoning, like, “Was that actually the right call, or am I just pretending I know what I’m doing?” So it slows me down a bit, and it makes everything feel heavier.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve kind of pulled back from people—friends, family—not because they did anything, but because I don’t really trust my mood or my judgment lately. I can recognize that, logically. But when it’s me, it feels different. I keep thinking, “You should know more by now,” or “You shouldn’t need to double-check this,” or “At your age and at this level, you shouldn’t feel this unsure.” It turns into this running commentary in my head where I’m not just worried about making a mistake—I’m judging myself for even having the worry.\n\nSo yeah, guilty or worthless… maybe “worthless” sounds strong, but there are definitely moments where I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people have put in me, and like I’m taking up a role that should belong to someone more solid or more naturally confident. Not every second, but enough that it feels like I’m carrying it around.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, dulled-out mood, and self-criticism — and has it been present most days for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… so, looking back, I’d say this has been hanging around off and on for about eleven months now. Not always at the exact same intensity, and there have been stretches where I’ve felt more like myself or at least more functional, but the low feeling and the guilt-y, self-critical stuff keeps coming back.\n\nAs far as whether it’s been most days for two weeks at a time… yeah, I think so. I’m not great at marking it on a calendar, but there have definitely been periods where for a couple weeks or more, most days had that same heaviness to them. It’s like I could still get through work and do what I needed to do, so part of me kept dismissing it, but emotionally it’s been there in the background a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry often comes up even when there’s no specific stressor or trigger, or does it always seem to be tied to a particular situation or event?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. It feels like I can usually find a reason if I look for one long enough, especially something work-related or some interaction I’m replaying in my head. But I don’t know if that means it was actually triggered by that, or if my brain just sort of grabs onto whatever’s nearby and uses it as the explanation.\n\nThere are times where I’ll just feel keyed up or uneasy and then I start scanning for why. Like, “Did I forget something? Is there an email I missed? Did I sound stupid in that meeting?” And sometimes there is a specific thing, but other times it’s more vague than that. It’s not always like, “This happened, so now I’m anxious.” Sometimes the worry seems to show up first, and then I attach it to something after the fact.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say it’s not always tied to one clear event, but I also have a hard time saying it comes out of nowhere, because I’m pretty good at making it about something once it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry often comes up even when there's no specific stressor or trigger related to work or other situations, or does it always seem to be tied to a particular situation or event?\n\nPatient: I think… I may have already sort of answered this, but I’m honestly still not completely sure how to tell the difference.\n\nIt doesn’t feel neatly tied to one thing every time. Like with work, that’s the easiest place my mind goes, because there’s always something I can point to if I try hard enough — an email, a meeting, whether I sounded competent, whether I missed some detail. But sometimes the uneasy feeling is there first, and then I start looking around for the reason. So I’ll kind of assign it to something afterward.\n\nI wouldn’t say it always comes out of nowhere, though. That feels too strong, because once I’m worried, I can usually find some situation to connect it to. But I also can’t say it’s always because of one clear event. It’s more like this background hum sometimes, and then my brain picks a target.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice physical symptoms, like restlessness, muscle tension, or trouble sleeping, especially when you're feeling more anxious or self-critical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the sleep part is definitely there. I don’t know if I’d say it happens every single night, but when I’m more keyed up or beating myself up about things, sleep gets kind of messed up.\n\nLately it’s been one of two versions. Either I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be awake — and my brain immediately starts running through client tickets or things I might’ve missed, and then I can’t really get back to sleep. It’s not even productive thinking, it’s just the same stuff looping.\n\nOr I’ll technically sleep enough hours, like on paper it should’ve been fine, but I wake up still feeling worn out, like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. So yeah, sleep has been tied into it, especially when I’m anxious or being hard on myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about other areas of your life outside of work, like your health, relationships, or things that might happen in the future, even when there’s no immediate reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. Work is the thing I notice most because it’s concrete and easy to point to. With stuff outside of work — like health or relationships or the future — I don’t immediately think, “Yes, that’s a big pattern,” but I also don’t know if I’ve been paying close enough attention to separate it out.\n\nI mean, I can worry about those things like anyone does, but I’m not sure I’d call it excessive, or at least I don’t have a specific example jumping out at me right now. It’s more that when I’m already uneasy, my mind can kind of look for something to attach to, and work is usually the easiest target. Outside of work, it’s less obvious to me. So… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed a strong pattern there, but I’m not totally certain.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things outside of work, like your health, relationships, or things that might happen in the future, even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think my answer is still kind of the same, though I’m sorry if I’m circling around it.\n\nI don’t really notice a clear pattern of worrying a lot about health or relationships or future stuff when there’s no reason. Not in the way I notice it with work. With work, I can usually name the thing I’m stuck on, even if it’s kind of irrational or bigger in my head than it needs to be. Outside of work, it’s harder for me to say.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I worry about normal life things sometimes, but I don’t have this immediate sense of, “Yes, I’m constantly worried about my health,” or “I’m always afraid something bad is going to happen.” That doesn’t really sound like what I’m aware of. It may be that I’m just not tracking it well, but if you’re asking what stands out to me, it’s mostly not those areas. Work is the place my mind seems to latch onto most easily.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t think so, not in a way I can clearly identify. Like, if I’m doing routine stuff — dishes, errands, making coffee, whatever — I don’t usually notice that I’m just anxious for no reason. I might be distracted or kind of in my head, but I don’t know that I’d label it as worry.

If something specific is going on, then sure, I can feel tense, but just sitting with someone or trying to relax, I’m not always aware of this background anxiety humming away. Or at least if it’s there, I’m not good at noticing it until it gets tied to something more concrete.

So… I’d say I haven’t really noticed a lot of anxiety when work isn’t in the picture. It may be happening in a more subtle way, but I can’t honestly say, “Yes, that’s a big thing for me.”

I work as a computer systems analyst, and I’ve been in a more senior-type role, or at least people seem to think I can handle that level of responsibility, and I just keep feeling like… like I tricked everyone somehow.\n\nI keep beating myself up, thinking I’m not actually “senior material,” that I’m not as capable as they think I am, and that eventually they’re going to realize I can’t handle as much as they believed. It’s not just a passing thought either—I keep turning it over and over, replaying things I said or decisions I made, and I end up feeling guilty, like I’ve misrepresented myself even though I didn’t mean to. That’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these kinds of worries extend beyond work? Like, do you often feel this level of self-doubt or anxiety in other areas of your life, such as relationships, personal responsibilities, or everyday decisions?\n\nPatient: I mean… it’s mostly centered on work, honestly. That’s the thing I keep circling back to. It’s not like I’m walking around questioning every single little thing in my whole life, but with this job and the responsibilities people think I can handle, it really gets under my skin.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m not really senior material, like I somehow gave people the wrong impression, or they saw something in me that isn’t actually there. And then I feel guilty about it, like I fooled everyone into trusting me with more than I can actually manage. I know that probably sounds kind of harsh, because nobody is accusing me of anything, but in my own head it feels like I’m waiting to be found out.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain tasks, responsibilities, or even interactions at work because of these worries about being seen as inadequate or a fraud?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped doing the job or anything—I’m still showing up, still getting my work done, still answering messages and going to meetings. But I’ve noticed I’m a lot less… confident, I guess, in how I move through things.\n\nLike, I’ll finish something and then go back over it more than I normally would, sometimes several times, because I don’t trust that I caught everything or that my judgment was right the first time. Even with decisions I’ve made plenty of times before, I’ll hesitate and sort of second-guess the reasoning, like, “Was that actually the right call, or am I just pretending I know what I’m doing?” So it slows me down a bit, and it makes everything feel heavier.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve kind of pulled back from people—friends, family—not because they did anything, but because I don’t really trust my mood or my judgment lately. I worry I’ll say something off, or I won’t be present, or I’ll make things awkward. So I’ve been keeping more to myself. It’s not like my whole life has fallen apart, but I can feel myself withdrawing and overchecking things more than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry tends to come and go depending on specific stressors, or does it feel more constant, like it’s always lingering in the background even when things are going okay?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that, honestly. It feels like it’s tied to work a lot of the time, or at least that’s what I notice most clearly, because that’s where the “I’m going to be found out” kind of feeling shows up.\n\nBut then even when nothing specific is happening—like no big meeting, no deadline, no one criticizing me—it can still kind of sit there in the background. Not always at the same volume, I guess. Some days it’s more like a low hum, and I can go through the day and function, and other days something small will kick it up, like an email from someone higher up or having to give an opinion, and then I start turning it over again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it constant exactly, because there are moments when I’m distracted or things feel okay. But it’s also not only when there’s a clear crisis. It sort of lingers, especially around the job stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed changes in your mood, like feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, especially alongside these worries about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. It’s not like I wake up every single morning thinking, “I’m hopeless,” exactly, but most days lately there’s been this heavy, low feeling sitting on me. Like I’m going through the motions at work and then coming home and doing the basic things I’m supposed to do, but everything feels kind of dulled out.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is like the color’s been turned down on things. I’m still functioning, I’m still doing what I need to do, but there’s not much brightness to it. And with the work worries layered on top, it’s like even normal parts of the day feel heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or worthless, even when there’s no clear reason or when others wouldn’t see the situation the same way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I keep beating myself up over this idea that I’m not really senior material at work, like I somehow got put in a position people think I can handle, but I’ve fooled them into believing I’m more capable than I actually am.\n\nAnd the thing is, if someone else described the same situation to me, I’d probably tell them they’re being way too hard on themselves. I can recognize that, logically. But when it’s me, it feels different. I keep thinking, “You should know more by now,” or “You shouldn’t need to double-check this,” or “At your age and at this level, you shouldn’t feel this unsure.” It turns into this running commentary in my head where I’m not just worried about making a mistake—I’m judging myself for even having the worry.\n\nSo yeah, guilty or worthless… maybe “worthless” sounds strong, but there are definitely moments where I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people have put in me, and like I’m taking up a role that should belong to someone more solid or more naturally confident. It’s embarrassing to say out loud, because nobody has really said that to me directly. It’s more like I’ve decided it about myself and then I keep looking for proof.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling guilty or worthless even when you haven’t done anything specific to cause those feelings, like they’re just there regardless of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can usually attach it to work if I think long enough, but it’s not always like I did one specific thing wrong and then I feel guilty. A lot of the time it’s more like this general feeling that I’m not what people think I am.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep beating myself up over this idea that I’m not really senior material, like I somehow fooled everyone into thinking I can handle more responsibility than I actually can. And nobody is necessarily saying that to me. It’s more internal. I’ll just be sitting there, even if the day is going basically fine, and I’ll start thinking, “You shouldn’t be in this role,” or “They’re going to realize you’re not as capable as they thought.”\n\nSo yes, it can feel like it’s just there regardless of what’s happening around me. Not every second, but enough that it feels like I’m carrying it around.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, dulled-out mood, and self-criticism — and has it been present most days for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… so, looking back, I’d say this has been hanging around off and on for about eleven months now. Not always at the exact same intensity, and there have been stretches where I’ve felt more like myself or at least more functional, but the low feeling and the guilt-y, self-critical stuff keeps coming back.\n\nAs far as whether it’s been most days for two weeks at a time… yeah, I think so. I’m not great at marking it on a calendar, but there have definitely been periods where for a couple weeks or more, most days had that same heaviness to them. It’s like I could still get through work and do what I needed to do, so part of me kept dismissing it, but emotionally it’s been there in the background a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry often comes up even when there’s no specific stressor or trigger, or does it always seem to be tied to a particular situation or event?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. It feels like I can usually find a reason if I look for one long enough, especially something work-related or some interaction I’m replaying in my head. But I don’t know if that means it was actually triggered by that, or if my brain just sort of grabs onto whatever’s nearby and uses it as the explanation.\n\nThere are times where I’ll just feel keyed up or uneasy and then I start scanning for why. Like, “Did I forget something? Is there an email I missed? Did I sound stupid in that meeting?” And sometimes there is a specific thing, but other times it’s more vague than that. It’s not always like, “This happened, so now I’m anxious.” Sometimes the worry seems to show up first, and then I attach it to something after the fact.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say it’s not always tied to one clear event, but I also have a hard time saying it comes out of nowhere, because I’m pretty good at making it about something once it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry often comes up even when there's no specific stressor or trigger related to work or other situations, or does it always seem to be tied to a particular situation or event?\n\nPatient: I think… I may have already sort of answered this, but I’m honestly still not completely sure how to tell the difference.\n\nIt doesn’t feel neatly tied to one thing every time. Like with work, that’s the easiest place my mind goes, because there’s always something I can point to if I try hard enough — an email, a meeting, whether I sounded competent, whether I missed some detail. But sometimes the uneasy feeling is there first, and then I start looking around for the reason. So I’ll kind of assign it to something afterward.\n\nI wouldn’t say it always comes out of nowhere, though. That feels too strong, because once I’m worried, I can usually find some situation to connect it to. But I also can’t say it’s always because of one clear event. It’s more like this background hum sometimes, and then my brain picks a target.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice physical symptoms, like restlessness, muscle tension, or trouble sleeping, especially when you're feeling more anxious or self-critical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the sleep part is definitely there. I don’t know if I’d say it happens every single night, but when I’m more keyed up or beating myself up about things, sleep gets kind of messed up.\n\nLately it’s been one of two versions. Either I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be awake — and my brain immediately starts running through client tickets or things I might’ve missed, and then I can’t really get back to sleep. It’s not even productive thinking, it’s just the same stuff looping.\n\nOr I’ll technically sleep enough hours, like on paper it should’ve been fine, but I wake up still feeling worn out, like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. So yeah, sleep has been tied into it, especially when I’m anxious or being hard on myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about other areas of your life outside of work, like your health, relationships, or things that might happen in the future, even when there’s no immediate reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. Work is the thing I notice most because it’s concrete and easy to point to. With stuff outside of work — like health or relationships or the future — I don’t immediately think, “Yes, that’s a big pattern,” but I also don’t know if I’ve been paying close enough attention to separate it out.\n\nI mean, I can worry about those things like anyone does, but I’m not sure I’d call it excessive, or at least I don’t have a specific example jumping out at me right now. It’s more that when I’m already uneasy, my mind can kind of look for something to attach to, and work is usually the easiest target. Outside of work, it’s less obvious to me. So… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed a strong pattern there, but I’m not totally certain.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things outside of work, like your health, relationships, or things that might happen in the future, even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think my answer is still kind of the same, though I’m sorry if I’m circling around it.\n\nI don’t really notice a clear pattern of worrying a lot about health or relationships or future stuff when there’s no reason. Not in the way I notice it with work. With work, I can usually name the thing I’m stuck on, even if it’s kind of irrational or bigger in my head than it needs to be. Outside of work, it’s harder for me to say.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I worry about normal life things sometimes, but I don’t have this immediate sense of, “Yes, I’m constantly worried about my health,” or “I’m always afraid something bad is going to happen.” That doesn’t really sound like what I’m aware of. It may be that I’m just not tracking it well, but if you’re asking what stands out to me, it’s mostly not those areas. Work is the place my mind seems to latch onto most easily.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried even when you're not thinking about work, such as when you're doing routine tasks, spending time with others, or just relaxing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way I can clearly identify. Like, if I’m doing routine stuff — dishes, errands, making coffee, whatever — I don’t usually notice that I’m just anxious for no reason. I might be distracted or kind of in my head, but I don’t know that I’d label it as worry.\n\nWith other people, it depends a little, I guess. If something specific is going on, then sure, I can feel tense, but just sitting with someone or trying to relax, I’m not always aware of this background anxiety humming away. Or at least if it’s there, I’m not good at noticing it until it gets tied to something more concrete.\n\nSo… I’d say I haven’t really noticed a lot of anxiety when work isn’t in the picture. It may be happening in a more subtle way, but I can’t honestly say, “Yes, that’s a big thing for me.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have noticed that, actually. It’s not like I suddenly hate everything, but the stuff I’d normally kind of look forward to just doesn’t have the same pull.

Like, weekends are usually when I’ll grill something, even if it’s nothing fancy, just because I enjoy messing with it and being outside. Or I’ll think about going fishing, getting the gear together, even if I don’t catch much. And I’ve always liked putting on a record and just sitting with it for a while. But lately it’s like… the grill is there, the fishing stuff is there, the records are right there, and I just kind of leave all of it sitting. I’ll think, “I could do that,” and then there’s no real interest behind it. I don’t feel excited about it.

It’s a strange thing because those are small things, but they’re usually my small things, if that makes sense. And lately I just don’t really care enough to start.

I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped doing the job or anything—I’m still showing up, still getting my work done, still answering messages and going to meetings. But I’ve noticed I’m a lot less… confident, I guess, in how I move through things.\n\nLike, I’ll finish something and then go back over it more than I normally would, sometimes several times, because I don’t trust that I caught everything or that my judgment was right the first time. Even with decisions I’ve made plenty of times before, I’ll hesitate and sort of second-guess the reasoning, like, “Was that actually the right call, or am I just pretending I know what I’m doing?” So it slows me down a bit, and it makes everything feel heavier.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve kind of pulled back from people—friends, family—not because they did anything, but because I don’t really trust my mood or my judgment lately. I worry I’ll say something off, or I won’t be present, or I’ll make things awkward. So I’ve been keeping more to myself. It’s not like my whole life has fallen apart, but I can feel myself withdrawing and overchecking things more than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry tends to come and go depending on specific stressors, or does it feel more constant, like it’s always lingering in the background even when things are going okay?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that, honestly. It feels like it’s tied to work a lot of the time, or at least that’s what I notice most clearly, because that’s where the “I’m going to be found out” kind of feeling shows up.\n\nBut then even when nothing specific is happening—like no big meeting, no deadline, no one criticizing me—it can still kind of sit there in the background. Not always at the same volume, I guess. Some days it’s more like a low hum, and I can go through the day and function, and other days something small will kick it up, like an email from someone higher up or having to give an opinion, and then I start turning it over again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it constant exactly, because there are moments when I’m distracted or things feel okay. But it’s also not only when there’s a clear crisis. It sort of lingers, especially around the job stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed changes in your mood, like feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, especially alongside these worries about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. It’s not like I wake up every single morning thinking, “I’m hopeless,” exactly, but most days lately there’s been this heavy, low feeling sitting on me. Like I’m going through the motions at work and then coming home and doing the basic things I’m supposed to do, but everything feels kind of dulled out.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is like the color’s been turned down on things. I’m still functioning, I’m still doing what I need to do, but there’s not much brightness to it. And with the work worries layered on top, it’s like even normal parts of the day feel heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or worthless, even when there’s no clear reason or when others wouldn’t see the situation the same way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I keep beating myself up over this idea that I’m not really senior material at work, like I somehow got put in a position people think I can handle, but I’ve fooled them into believing I’m more capable than I actually am.\n\nAnd the thing is, if someone else described the same situation to me, I’d probably tell them they’re being way too hard on themselves. I can recognize that, logically. But when it’s me, it feels different. I keep thinking, “You should know more by now,” or “You shouldn’t need to double-check this,” or “At your age and at this level, you shouldn’t feel this unsure.” It turns into this running commentary in my head where I’m not just worried about making a mistake—I’m judging myself for even having the worry.\n\nSo yeah, guilty or worthless… maybe “worthless” sounds strong, but there are definitely moments where I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people have put in me, and like I’m taking up a role that should belong to someone more solid or more naturally confident. It’s embarrassing to say out loud, because nobody has really said that to me directly. It’s more like I’ve decided it about myself and then I keep looking for proof.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling guilty or worthless even when you haven’t done anything specific to cause those feelings, like they’re just there regardless of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can usually attach it to work if I think long enough, but it’s not always like I did one specific thing wrong and then I feel guilty. A lot of the time it’s more like this general feeling that I’m not what people think I am.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep beating myself up over this idea that I’m not really senior material, like I somehow fooled everyone into thinking I can handle more responsibility than I actually can. And nobody is necessarily saying that to me. It’s more internal. I’ll just be sitting there, even if the day is going basically fine, and I’ll start thinking, “You shouldn’t be in this role,” or “They’re going to realize you’re not as capable as they thought.”\n\nSo yes, it can feel like it’s just there regardless of what’s happening around me. Not every second, but enough that it feels like I’m carrying it around.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, dulled-out mood, and self-criticism — and has it been present most days for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… so, looking back, I’d say this has been hanging around off and on for about eleven months now. Not always at the exact same intensity, and there have been stretches where I’ve felt more like myself or at least more functional, but the low feeling and the guilt-y, self-critical stuff keeps coming back.\n\nAs far as whether it’s been most days for two weeks at a time… yeah, I think so. I’m not great at marking it on a calendar, but there have definitely been periods where for a couple weeks or more, most days had that same heaviness to them. It’s like I could still get through work and do what I needed to do, so part of me kept dismissing it, but emotionally it’s been there in the background a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry often comes up even when there’s no specific stressor or trigger, or does it always seem to be tied to a particular situation or event?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. It feels like I can usually find a reason if I look for one long enough, especially something work-related or some interaction I’m replaying in my head. But I don’t know if that means it was actually triggered by that, or if my brain just sort of grabs onto whatever’s nearby and uses it as the explanation.\n\nThere are times where I’ll just feel keyed up or uneasy and then I start scanning for why. Like, “Did I forget something? Is there an email I missed? Did I sound stupid in that meeting?” And sometimes there is a specific thing, but other times it’s more vague than that. It’s not always like, “This happened, so now I’m anxious.” Sometimes the worry seems to show up first, and then I attach it to something after the fact.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say it’s not always tied to one clear event, but I also have a hard time saying it comes out of nowhere, because I’m pretty good at making it about something once it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety or worry often comes up even when there's no specific stressor or trigger related to work or other situations, or does it always seem to be tied to a particular situation or event?\n\nPatient: I think… I may have already sort of answered this, but I’m honestly still not completely sure how to tell the difference.\n\nIt doesn’t feel neatly tied to one thing every time. Like with work, that’s the easiest place my mind goes, because there’s always something I can point to if I try hard enough — an email, a meeting, whether I sounded competent, whether I missed some detail. But sometimes the uneasy feeling is there first, and then I start looking around for the reason. So I’ll kind of assign it to something afterward.\n\nI wouldn’t say it always comes out of nowhere, though. That feels too strong, because once I’m worried, I can usually find some situation to connect it to. But I also can’t say it’s always because of one clear event. It’s more like this background hum sometimes, and then my brain picks a target.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice physical symptoms, like restlessness, muscle tension, or trouble sleeping, especially when you're feeling more anxious or self-critical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the sleep part is definitely there. I don’t know if I’d say it happens every single night, but when I’m more keyed up or beating myself up about things, sleep gets kind of messed up.\n\nLately it’s been one of two versions. Either I wake up way too early — like before I actually need to be awake — and my brain immediately starts running through client tickets or things I might’ve missed, and then I can’t really get back to sleep. It’s not even productive thinking, it’s just the same stuff looping.\n\nOr I’ll technically sleep enough hours, like on paper it should’ve been fine, but I wake up still feeling worn out, like the sleep didn’t really do what it was supposed to do. So yeah, sleep has been tied into it, especially when I’m anxious or being hard on myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about other areas of your life outside of work, like your health, relationships, or things that might happen in the future, even when there’s no immediate reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. Work is the thing I notice most because it’s concrete and easy to point to. With stuff outside of work — like health or relationships or the future — I don’t immediately think, “Yes, that’s a big pattern,” but I also don’t know if I’ve been paying close enough attention to separate it out.\n\nI mean, I can worry about those things like anyone does, but I’m not sure I’d call it excessive, or at least I don’t have a specific example jumping out at me right now. It’s more that when I’m already uneasy, my mind can kind of look for something to attach to, and work is usually the easiest target. Outside of work, it’s less obvious to me. So… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed a strong pattern there, but I’m not totally certain.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things outside of work, like your health, relationships, or things that might happen in the future, even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think my answer is still kind of the same, though I’m sorry if I’m circling around it.\n\nI don’t really notice a clear pattern of worrying a lot about health or relationships or future stuff when there’s no reason. Not in the way I notice it with work. With work, I can usually name the thing I’m stuck on, even if it’s kind of irrational or bigger in my head than it needs to be. Outside of work, it’s harder for me to say.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I worry about normal life things sometimes, but I don’t have this immediate sense of, “Yes, I’m constantly worried about my health,” or “I’m always afraid something bad is going to happen.” That doesn’t really sound like what I’m aware of. It may be that I’m just not tracking it well, but if you’re asking what stands out to me, it’s mostly not those areas. Work is the place my mind seems to latch onto most easily.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried even when you're not thinking about work, such as when you're doing routine tasks, spending time with others, or just relaxing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way I can clearly identify. Like, if I’m doing routine stuff — dishes, errands, making coffee, whatever — I don’t usually notice that I’m just anxious for no reason. I might be distracted or kind of in my head, but I don’t know that I’d label it as worry.\n\nWith other people, it depends a little, I guess. If something specific is going on, then sure, I can feel tense, but just sitting with someone or trying to relax, I’m not always aware of this background anxiety humming away. Or at least if it’s there, I’m not good at noticing it until it gets tied to something more concrete.\n\nSo… I’d say I haven’t really noticed a lot of anxiety when work isn’t in the picture. It may be happening in a more subtle way, but I can’t honestly say, “Yes, that’s a big thing for me.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that, actually. It’s not like I suddenly hate everything, but the stuff I’d normally kind of look forward to just doesn’t have the same pull.\n\nLike, weekends are usually when I’ll grill something, even if it’s nothing fancy, just because I enjoy messing with it and being outside. Or I’ll think about going fishing, getting the gear together, even if I don’t catch much. And I’ve always liked putting on a record and just sitting with it for a while. But lately it’s like… the grill is there, the fishing stuff is there, the records are right there, and I just kind of leave all of it sitting. I’ll think, “I could do that,” and then there’s no real interest behind it. Yeah, I have noticed that. It’s not like I’m doing heavy labor or anything, you know, I’m mostly at a desk during the day, but by the time I get home from the office I feel kind of… used up.

And then it’s embarrassing how small the things are that feel like too much. Like checking the mail — it’s right there, it takes two minutes — but I’ll sit in the car or walk past it and think, “I’ll get it later,” because even that feels like another task. Or loading the dishwasher, which isn’t exactly difficult, but it feels like it takes more effort than it should. I’ll look at the plates in the sink and just feel tired before I even start.

So yeah, most days lately there’s been this lack of energy, even when I can’t really point to something that should have worn me out. It’s more like I get home and everything simple feels heavier than it ought to.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess the main thing is I’ve been really stuck in my own head lately. I work as a computer systems analyst, and I’ve been in a more senior-type role, or at least people seem to think I can handle that level of responsibility, and I just keep feeling like… like I tricked everyone somehow.\n\nI keep beating myself up, thinking I’m not actually “senior material,” that I’m not as capable as they think I am, and that eventually they’re going to realize I can’t handle as much as they believed. It’s not just a passing thought either—I keep turning it over and over, replaying things I said or decisions I made, and I end up feeling guilty, like I’ve misrepresented myself even though I didn’t mean to. That’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these kinds of worries extend beyond work? Like, do you often feel this level of self-doubt or anxiety in other areas of your life, such as relationships, personal responsibilities, or everyday decisions?\n\nPatient: I mean… it’s mostly centered on work, honestly. That’s the thing I keep circling back to. It’s not like I’m walking around questioning every single little thing in my whole life, but with this job and the responsibilities people think I can handle, it really gets under my skin.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m not really senior material, like I somehow gave people the wrong impression, or they saw something in me that isn’t actually there. And then I feel guilty about it, like I fooled everyone into trusting me with more than I can actually manage. I know that probably sounds kind of harsh, because nobody is accusing me of anything, but in my own head it feels like I’m waiting to be found out.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain tasks, responsibilities, or even interactions at work because of these worries about being seen as inadequate or a fraud?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped doing the job or anything—I’m still showing up, still getting my work done, still answering messages and going to meetings. Even with decisions I’ve made plenty of times before, I’ll hesitate and sort of second-guess the reasoning, like, “Was that actually the right call, or am I just pretending I know what I’m doing?” So it slows me down a bit, and it makes everything feel heavier.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve kind of pulled back from people—friends, family—not because they did anything, but because I don’t really trust my mood or my judgment lately. I worry I’ll say something off, or I won’t be present, or I’ll make things awkward. So I’ve been keeping more to myself.
